*   **The "C":** Instead of a standard curve, it looks like a staple or a bracket (`[`). It has a hard, straight horizontal line on top and a matching one on the bottom, connected by a vertical spine.
*   **The "D":** Placed immediately to the right. Depending on the font consistency, the "D" likely shares that same flat top and bottom height, but with a curved or angled front face.

**Visual Interpretations:**

1.  **The "Capsule" Look:** If the "C" and "D" are pushed close together, the flat lines of the C flow into the D, creating a shape that looks like a pill or a capsule with a vertical line separating the two halves.
2.  **The "Link" Look:** It resembles a chain link that has been broken or a magnetic connection.
3.  **The "Tech" Look:** This specific style (flat top/bottom C) is common in sci-fi fonts (like the NASA "worm" logo style) or automotive branding.

```text
|         |       |
|______   |______/
```

*   The overall silhouette becomes a **capsule** or a **pill shape** (flat on the left, round on the right).

*   **Inside the C:** You now have a fully enclosed rectangle.
*   **Inside the D:** You have the standard "D" shaped hole.
*   Separating them is the solid vertical bar of the D's spine.

Model: I have it pictured. Here is a breakdown of what that shape looks like and how it behaves geometrically and visually:

### 1. The "Invisible Square"
The easiest way to visualize this is to imagine a perfect square.
*   The horizontal line (the crossbar) traces the entire **top edge** of the square.
*   The vertical line (the stem) runs from the center of that top edge straight down to the **bottom edge** of the square.

Because both lines are the same length, this "T" fits perfectly inside a $1\times1$ box.

### 2. The Optical Illusion
Here is the interesting part about this specific shape: **It will not look equal.**

Due to the **Vertical-Horizontal Illusion**, the human brain almost always perceives vertical lines as being longer than horizontal lines of the exact same length.
*   If you were to draw this "T" with a ruler, the vertical stem would appear to be about **10–15% longer** than the top bar to the naked eye.
*   To make a "T" *look* like a perfect square to the eye, you actually have to make the horizontal bar slightly longer than the vertical stem.

### 3. Aesthetic "Feel"
Compared to a standard typographic "T" (like the one you are reading right now), this equal-length version would look:
*   **Squat and stout:** Standard fonts usually have a stem that is taller than the crossbar is wide to maintain elegance and readability.
*   **Top-heavy:** It would resemble a mallet or a sledgehammer standing on its handle.

### 4. Visual Representation

Now, from a design perspective, this is where it gets interesting. Standard typography usually adjusts the top loop of an "8" to be *slightly* smaller. This is an optical correction to make it *look* balanced, even though it isn't geometrically perfect. The equal-loop "8" appears top-heavy to the untrained eye.

It makes me think of other things: a snowman without a head, maybe some heavy dumbbells or a chain. It's a striking image, this perfectly symmetrical "8," a visual representation of balance that feels a little *unnatural*, in the best possible way.

**1. The Geometry**
It looks less like a handwritten number and more like a geometric construction. It is essentially two perfect circles stacked vertically, touching at a single tangent point in the center. It has perfect vertical and horizontal symmetry.

**2. The "Vertical Infinity"**
If you take the standard infinity symbol ($\infty$) and rotate it 90 degrees, you get exactly what you are describing. It represents a cycle that never ends, falling and rising in a perfect loop.

**3. The Optical Illusion (Why fonts don't do this)**
In typography, the number "8" is almost always drawn with the top loop slightly smaller than the bottom loop.
*   **Why?** Because of an optical illusion, if both loops are mathematically the same size, the top one actually looks *bigger* and "top-heavy" to the human eye.
*   **The Result:** The version you are imagining likely feels slightly unstable or "looming," as if it might tip over, compared to a standard printed 8.
